for some $h : Y \to Z'$ and $t : Z \to Z'$ in $S$. Using LMS2 we
choose morphisms $a : Y' \to Z''$ and $t' : Z' \to Z''$ in $S$ such
that $a \circ s = t' \circ h$. Picture
$$
\xymatrix{
& & Z \ar[d]^t \\
& Y \ar[r]^h \ar[d]^s & Z' \ar[d]^{t'} \\
X \ar[r]^{f, g} & Y' \ar[r]^a & Z''
}
$$
Then
$\gamma \circ \alpha = (t' \circ t)^{-1}(a \circ f)$ and
$\gamma \circ \beta = (t' \circ t)^{-1}(a \circ g)$.
Hence we see that $\gamma \circ (\alpha + \beta)$ is represented
by $(t' \circ t)^{-1}(a \circ (f + g)) =
(t' \circ t)^{-1}(a \circ f + a \circ g)$ which represents
$\gamma \circ \alpha + \gamma \circ \beta$.

\medskip\noindent
Finally, assume that $\delta : W \to X$ is another morphism of
$S^{-1}\mathcal{C}$. Say $\delta = r^{-1}i$ for some
$i : W \to X'$ and $r : X \to X'$ in $S$. We claim that we can find
a morphism $s' : Y' \to Y''$ in $S$ and morphisms $a'', b'' : X' \to Y''$
such that the following diagram commutes
$$
\xymatrix{
& & & Y \ar[d]^s \\
& X \ar[rr]^{f, g, f + g} \ar[d]^r & & Y' \ar[d]^{s'} \\
W \ar[r]^i & X' \ar[rr]^{a'', b'', a'' + b''} & & Y''
}
$$
Namely, using LMS2 we can first choose
$s_1 : Y' \to Y_1$, $s_2 : Y' \to Y_2$ in $S$ and
$a : X' \to Y_1$, $b : X' \to Y_2$ such that
$a \circ r = s_1 \circ f$ and $b \circ r = s_2 \circ f$.
Then using that the category $Y'/S$ is filtered (see
Categories, Remark \ref{categories-remark-left-localization-morphisms-colimit}),
we can
find a $s' : Y' \to Y''$ and morphisms $a' : Y_1 \to Y''$, $b' : Y_2 \to Y''$
such that $s' = a' \circ s_1$ and $s' = b' \circ s_2$. Setting
$a'' = a' \circ a$ and $b'' = b' \circ b$ works.
At this point we see that the compositions
$\alpha \circ \delta$ and $\beta \circ \delta$ are represented by
$(s' \circ s)^{-1}(a'' \circ i)$ and $(s' \circ s)^{-1}(b'' \circ i)$.
Hence $\alpha \circ \delta + \beta \circ \delta$ is represented
by $(s' \circ s)^{-1}(a'' \circ i + b'' \circ i) =
(s' \circ s)^{-1}((a'' + b'') \circ i)$
which by the diagram again is a representative
of $(\alpha + \beta) \circ \delta$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma-localization-additive}
Let $\mathcal{C}$ be an additive category.
Let $S$ be a left or right multiplicative system.
Then $S^{-1}\mathcal{C}$ is an additive category and the localization functor
$Q : \mathcal{C} \to S^{-1}\mathcal{C}$ is additive.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
By Lemma \ref{lemma-localization-preadditive}
we see that $S^{-1}\mathcal{C}$ is preadditive and that $Q$ is additive.
By Lemma \ref{lemma-additive-additive} we conclude that $S^{-1}\mathcal{C}$
has a zero object and direct sums.
\end{proof}

\noindent
The following lemma describes the ``kernel''
of the localization functor in case we invert a multiplicative system.

\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma-kernel-localization}
Let $\mathcal{C}$ be an additive category. Let $S$ be a multiplicative
system. Let $X$ be an object
of $\mathcal{C}$. The following are equivalent
\begin{enumerate}
\item $Q(X) = 0$ in $S^{-1}\mathcal{C}$,
\item there exists $Y \in \Ob(\mathcal{C})$ such that
$0 : X \to Y$ is an element of $S$, and
\item there exists $Z \in \Ob(\mathcal{C})$ such that
$0 : Z \to X$ is an element of $S$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
If (2) holds we see that $0 = Q(0) : Q(X) \to Q(Y)$ is an isomorphism.
In the additive category $S^{-1}\mathcal{C}$ this implies that $Q(X) = 0$.
Hence (2) $\Rightarrow$ (1). Similarly, (3) $\Rightarrow$ (1).
Suppose that $Q(X) = 0$. This implies that the morphism
$f : 0 \to X$ is transformed into an isomorphism in $S^{-1}\mathcal{C}$.
Hence by
Categories, Lemma \ref{categories-lemma-what-gets-inverted}
there exists a morphism $g : Z \to 0$ such that $fg \in S$. This proves
(1) $\Rightarrow$ (3). Similarly, (1) $\Rightarrow$ (2).
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma-localization-abelian}
Let $\mathcal{A}$ be an abelian category.
\begin{enumerate}
\item If $S$ is a left multiplicative system, then
the category $S^{-1}\mathcal{A}$ has cokernels and the functor
$Q : \mathcal{A} \to S^{-1}\mathcal{A}$ commutes with them.
\item If $S$ is a right multiplicative system, then
the category $S^{-1}\mathcal{A}$ has kernels and the functor
$Q : \mathcal{A} \to S^{-1}\mathcal{A}$ commutes with them.
\item If $S$ is a multiplicative system, then the category
$S^{-1}\mathcal{A}$ is abelian and the functor
$Q : \mathcal{A} \to S^{-1}\mathcal{A}$ is exact.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Assume $S$ is a left multiplicative system. Let $a : X \to Y$ be a morphism
of $S^{-1}\mathcal{A}$. Then $a = s^{-1}f$ for some $s : Y \to Y'$
in $S$ and $f : X \to Y'$. Since $Q(s)$ is an isomorphism we see that
the existence of $\Coker(a : X \to Y)$ is equivalent to the existence
of $\Coker(Q(f) : X \to Y')$. Since $\Coker(Q(f))$ is the
coequalizer of $0$ and $Q(f)$ we see that $\Coker(Q(f))$ is
represented by $Q(\Coker(f))$ by
Categories, Lemma \ref{categories-lemma-left-localization-limits}.
This proves (1).

\medskip\noindent
Part (2) is dual to part (1).

\medskip\noindent
If $S$ is a multiplicative system, then $S$ is both a left and a right
multiplicative system. Thus we see that $S^{-1}\mathcal{A}$ has
kernels and cokernels and $Q$ commutes with kernels and cokernels.
To finish the proof of (3) we have to show that $\Coim = \Im$ in
$S^{-1}\mathcal{A}$. Again using that any arrow in $S^{-1}\mathcal{A}$
is isomorphic to an arrow $Q(f)$ we see that the result follows
from the result for $\mathcal{A}$.
\end{proof}




\section{Jordan-H\"older}
\label{section-jordan-holder}

\noindent
The Jordan-H\"older lemma is Lemma \ref{lemma-jordan-holder}.
First we state some definitions.

\begin{definition}
\label{definition-simple}
Let $\mathcal{A}$ be an abelian category. An object $A$ of $\mathcal{A}$
is said to be {\it simple} if it is nonzero and the only subobjects
of $A$ are $0$ and $A$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
\label{definition-Artinian}
Let $\mathcal{A}$ be an abelian category.
\begin{enumerate}
\item We say an object $A$ of $\mathcal{A}$ is {\it Artinian} if and only if
it satisfies the descending chain condition for subobjects.
\item We say $\mathcal{A}$ is {\it Artinian} if every object of
$\mathcal{A}$ is Artinian.
\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
\label{definition-Noetherian}
Let $\mathcal{A}$ be an abelian category.
\begin{enumerate}
\item We say an object $A$ of $\mathcal{A}$ is {\it Noetherian} if and only if
it satisfies the ascending chain condition for subobjects.
\item We say $\mathcal{A}$ is {\it Noetherian} if every object of
$\mathcal{A}$ is Noetherian.
\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma-ses-artinian}
Let $\mathcal{A}$ be an abelian category. Let $0 \to A_1 \to A_2 \to A_3 \to 0$
be a short exact sequence of $\mathcal{A}$. Then $A_2$ is Artinian
if and only if $A_1$ and $A_3$ are Artinian.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Omitted.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma-ses-noetherian}
Let $\mathcal{A}$ be an abelian category. Let $0 \to A_1 \to A_2 \to A_3 \to 0$
be a short exact sequence of $\mathcal{A}$. Then $A_2$ is Noetherian
if and only if $A_1$ and $A_3$ are Noetherian.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Omitted.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma-finite-length}
Let $\mathcal{A}$ be an abelian category. Let $A$ be an object
of $\mathcal{A}$. The following are equivalent
\begin{enumerate}
\item $A$ is Artinian and Noetherian, and
\item there exists a filtration
$0 \subset A_1 \subset A_2 \subset \ldots \subset A_n = A$
by subobjects such that $A_i/A_{i - 1}$ is simple for $i = 1, \ldots, n$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Assume (1). If $A$ is zero, then (2) holds. If $A$ is not zero, then
there exists a smallest nonzero object $A_1 \subset A$ by the Artinian
property. Of course $A_1$ is simple. If $A_1 = A$, then we are done.
If not, then we can find $A_1 \subset A_2 \subset A$ minimal
with $A_2 \not = A_1$. Then $A_2/A_1$ is simple. Continuing in this way, we
can find a sequence $0 \subset A_1 \subset A_2 \subset \ldots $
of subobjects of $A$ such that $A_i/A_{i - 1}$ is simple. Since $A$
is Noetherian, we conclude that the process stops. Hence (2) follows.

\medskip\noindent
Assume (2). We will prove (1) by induction on $n$. If $n = 1$, then
$A$ is simple and clearly Noetherian and Artinian. If the result holds
for $n - 1$, then we use the short exact sequence
$0 \to A_{n - 1} \to A_n \to A_n/A_{n - 1} \to 0$
and Lemmas \ref{lemma-ses-artinian} and \ref{lemma-ses-noetherian}
to conclude for $n$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Jordan-H\"older]
\label{lemma-jordan-holder}
Let $\mathcal{A}$ be an abelian category. Let $A$ be an object
of $\mathcal{A}$ satisfying the equivalent conditions of
Lemma \ref{lemma-finite-length}. Given two filtrations
$$
0 \subset A_1 \subset A_2 \subset \ldots \subset A_n = A
\quad\text{and}\quad
0 \subset B_1 \subset B_2 \subset \ldots \subset B_m = A
$$
with $S_i = A_i/A_{i - 1}$ and $T_j = B_j/B_{j - 1}$ simple objects we have
$n = m$ and there exists a permutation $\sigma$ of $\{1, \ldots, n\}$
such that $S_i \cong T_{\sigma(i)}$ for all $i \in \{1, \ldots, n\}$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $j$ be the smallest index such that $A_1 \subset B_j$.
Then the map $S_1 = A_1 \to B_j/B_{j - 1} = T_j$ is an isomorphism.
Moreover, the object $A/A_1 = A_n/A_1 = B_m/A_1$
has the two filtrations
$$
0 \subset A_2/A_1 \subset A_3/A_1 \subset \ldots \subset A_n/A_1
$$
and
$$
0 \subset (B_1 + A_1)/A_1 \subset \ldots \subset
(B_{j - 1} + A_1)/A_1 = B_j/A_1 \subset B_{j + 1}/A_1
\subset \ldots \subset B_m/A_1
$$
We conclude by induction.
\end{proof}








\section{Serre subcategories}
\label{section-serre-subcategories}

\noindent
In \cite[Chapter I, Section 1]{Serre_homotopie_classes}
a notion of a ``class'' of abelian groups is defined.
This notion has been extended to abelian categories by many authors
(in slightly different ways). We will use the following variant
which is virtually identical to Serre's original definition.

\begin{definition}
\label{definition-serre-subcategory}
\begin{reference}
\cite[Condition (I) on page 259]{Serre_homotopie_classes}
\cite[Chapter III, \S 2, no. 56, p. 249]{FAC}
\end{reference}
\begin{history}
The cited FAC passage applies Serre's original notion of a class to the
graded modules whose components vanish in all sufficiently large degrees.
Its closure statement for an exact sequence $A \to B \to C$ is exactly the
condition in (1). The cutoff is uniform for the module; it should not be
confused, for an infinitely generated module, with the weaker assertion
that every element is killed by some power of an irrelevant ideal.
\end{history}
Let $\mathcal{A}$ be an abelian category.
\begin{enumerate}
\item A {\it Serre subcategory} of $\mathcal{A}$ is a
nonempty full subcategory $\mathcal{C}$ of $\mathcal{A}$
such that given an exact sequence\footnote{By
Definition \ref{definition-exact} this means $\Im(A \to B) = \Ker(B \to C)$.}
$$
A \to B \to C
$$
with $A, C \in \Ob(\mathcal{C})$, then also
$B \in \Ob(\mathcal{C})$.
\item A {\it weak Serre subcategory} of $\mathcal{A}$ is a nonempty
full subcategory $\mathcal{C}$ of $\mathcal{A}$ such that given an
exact sequence
$$
A_0 \to A_1 \to A_2 \to A_3 \to A_4
$$
with $A_0, A_1, A_3, A_4$ in $\mathcal{C}$, then also $A_2$ in $\mathcal{C}$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\noindent
In some references the second notion is called a ``thick'' subcategory
and in other references the first notion is called a ``thick'' subcategory.
However, it seems that the notion of a Serre subcategory is universally
accepted to be the one defined above. Note that in both cases the category
$\mathcal{C}$ is abelian and that the inclusion functor
$\mathcal{C} \to \mathcal{A}$ is a fully faithful exact functor.
Let's characterize these types of subcategories in more detail.

\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma-characterize-serre-subcategory}
Let $\mathcal{A}$ be an abelian category.
Let $\mathcal{C}$ be a subcategory of $\mathcal{A}$.
Then $\mathcal{C}$ is a Serre subcategory if and only if
the following conditions are satisfied:
\begin{enumerate}
\item $0 \in \Ob(\mathcal{C})$,
\item $\mathcal{C}$ is a strictly full subcategory of $\mathcal{A}$,
\item any subobject or quotient of an object of $\mathcal{C}$ is an object
of $\mathcal{C}$,
\item if $A \in \Ob(\mathcal{A})$ is an extension of objects of $\mathcal{C}$
then also $A \in \Ob(\mathcal{C})$.
\end{enumerate}
Moreover, a Serre subcategory is an abelian category and
the inclusion functor is exact.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Omitted.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma-characterize-weak-serre-subcategory}
Let $\mathcal{A}$ be an abelian category.
Let $\mathcal{C}$ be a subcategory of $\mathcal{A}$.
Then $\mathcal{C}$ is a weak Serre subcategory if and only if
the following conditions are satisfied:
\begin{enumerate}
\item $0 \in \Ob(\mathcal{C})$,
\item $\mathcal{C}$ is a strictly full subcategory of $\mathcal{A}$,
\item kernels and cokernels in $\mathcal{A}$ of morphisms
between objects of $\mathcal{C}$ are in $\mathcal{C}$,
\item if $A \in \Ob(\mathcal{A})$ is an extension of objects of $\mathcal{C}$
then also $A \in \Ob(\mathcal{C})$.
\end{enumerate}
Moreover, a weak Serre subcategory is an abelian category and
the inclusion functor is exact.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Omitted.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma-kernel-exact-functor}
Let $\mathcal{A}$, $\mathcal{B}$ be abelian categories.
Let $F : \mathcal{A} \to \mathcal{B}$ be an exact functor.
Then the full subcategory of objects $C$ of $\mathcal{A}$
such that $F(C) = 0$ forms a Serre subcategory of $\mathcal{A}$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Omitted.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}
\label{definition-kernel-category}
Let $\mathcal{A}$, $\mathcal{B}$ be abelian categories.
Let $F : \mathcal{A} \to \mathcal{B}$ be an exact functor.
Then the full subcategory of objects $C$ of $\mathcal{A}$
such that $F(C) = 0$ is called the {\it kernel of the functor $F$},
and is sometimes denoted $\Ker(F)$.
\end{definition}

\noindent
Any Serre subcategory of an abelian category is the kernel of
an exact functor. In
Examples, Section \ref{examples-section-serre-quotient-modulo-torsion-modules}
we discuss this for Serre's original example of torsion groups.

\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma-serre-subcategory-is-kernel}
\begin{reference}
\cite[Chapter III, \S 2, no. 56, p. 249]{FAC}
\end{reference}
\begin{history}
The cited source calls a morphism $\mathcal{C}$-injective or
$\mathcal{C}$-surjective when its kernel or cokernel, respectively, lies in
$\mathcal{C}$, and $\mathcal{C}$-bijective when both conditions hold. These
are exactly the arrows in the multiplicative system $S$ used below; they
become isomorphisms in the Serre quotient, but need not be isomorphisms in
the original category.
\end{history}
Let $\mathcal{A}$ be an abelian category.
Let $\mathcal{C} \subset \mathcal{A}$ be a Serre subcategory.
There exists an abelian category $\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{C}$
and an exact functor
$$
F : \mathcal{A} \longrightarrow \mathcal{A}/\mathcal{C}
$$
which is essentially surjective and whose kernel is $\mathcal{C}$.
The category $\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{C}$ and the functor $F$ are
characterized by the following universal property: For any exact
functor $G : \mathcal{A} \to \mathcal{B}$ such that
$\mathcal{C} \subset \Ker(G)$ there exists a factorization
$G = H \circ F$ for a unique exact functor
$H : \mathcal{A}/\mathcal{C} \to \mathcal{B}$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Consider the set of arrows of $\mathcal{A}$ defined by
the following formula
$$
S = \{f \in \text{Arrows}(\mathcal{A}) \mid
\Ker(f), \Coker(f) \in \Ob(\mathcal{C}) \}.
$$
We claim that $S$ is a multiplicative system. To prove this we have
to check MS1, MS2, MS3, see
Categories, Definition \ref{categories-definition-multiplicative-system}.

\medskip\noindent
It is clear that identities are elements of $S$. Suppose that
$f : A \to B$ and $g : B \to C$ are elements of $S$.
There are exact sequences
$$
\begin{matrix}
0 \to \Ker(f) \to \Ker(gf) \to \Ker(g) \\
\Coker(f) \to \Coker(gf) \to \Coker(g) \to 0
\end{matrix}
$$
Hence it follows that $gf \in S$. This proves MS1. (In fact, a similar
argument will show that $S$ is a saturated multiplicative system, see
Categories, Definition
\ref{categories-definition-saturated-multiplicative-system}.)

\medskip\noindent
Consider a solid diagram
$$
\xymatrix{
A \ar[d]_t \ar[r]_g & B \ar@{..>}[d]^s \\
C \ar@{..>}[r]^f & C \amalg_A B
}
$$
with $t \in S$. Set
$W = C \amalg_A B =  \Coker((t, -g) : A \to C \oplus B)$.
Then $\Ker(t) \to \Ker(s)$ is surjective and
$\Coker(t) \to \Coker(s)$ is an isomorphism. Hence
$s$ is an element of $S$. This proves LMS2 and the proof of RMS2 is dual.

\medskip\noindent
Finally, consider morphisms $f, g : B \to C$ and a morphism $s : A \to B$
in $S$ such that $f \circ s = g \circ s$. This means that
$(f - g) \circ s = 0$. In turn this means that
$I = \Im(f - g) \subset C$ is a quotient of $\Coker(s)$
hence an object of $\mathcal{C}$. Thus $t : C \to C' = C/I$ is an
element of $S$ such that $t \circ (f - g) = 0$, i.e., such that
$t \circ f = t \circ g$. This proves LMS3 and the proof of
RMS3 is dual.

\medskip\noindent
Having proved that $S$ is a multiplicative system we set
$\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{C} = S^{-1}\mathcal{A}$, and we set
$F$ equal to the localization functor $Q$. By
Lemma \ref{lemma-localization-abelian}
the category $\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{C}$ is abelian and $F$ is exact.
If $X$ is in the kernel of $F = Q$, then by
Lemma \ref{lemma-kernel-localization}
we see that $0 : X \to Z$ is an element of $S$ and hence
$X$ is an object of $\mathcal{C}$, i.e., the kernel of
$F$ is $\mathcal{C}$.
Finally, if $G$ is as in the statement of the lemma, then $G$ turns
every element of $S$ into an isomorphism. Hence we obtain the
functor $H : \mathcal{A}/\mathcal{C} \to \mathcal{B}$ from
the universal property of localization, see
Categories, Lemma \ref{categories-lemma-properties-left-localization}.
We still have to show the functor $H$ is exact.
To do this it suffices to show that $H$ commutes
with taking kernels and cokernels, see Lemma \ref{lemma-exact-functor}.
Let $A \to B$ be a morphism in $\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{C}$.
We may represent $A \to B$ as $fs^{-1}$ where $s : A' \to A$
is in $S$ and $f : A' \to B$ an arbitrary morphism of $\mathcal{A}$.
Since $F = Q$ maps $s$ to an isomorphism in the quotient category
$\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{C}$, it suffices to show that $H$ commutes with taking
kernels and cokernels of morphisms $f : A \to B$ of $\mathcal{A}$.
But here we have $H(f) = G(f)$ and the result follows
from the fact that $G$ is exact.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}
\label{definition-serre-quasi-equal}
\begin{reference}
\cite[Chapter I, \S 1.11, p. 138]{Tohoku}
\end{reference}
Let $\mathcal{A}$ be an abelian category and let
$\mathcal{C} \subset \mathcal{A}$ be a Serre subcategory.
\begin{enumerate}
\item A subobject $A' \subset A$ is \emph{quasi-equal to $A$ modulo
$\mathcal{C}$} if $A/A'$ is an object of $\mathcal{C}$.
\item A quotient $B \to B/N$ is \emph{quasi-equal to $B$ modulo
$\mathcal{C}$} if $N$ is an object of $\mathcal{C}$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma-serre-quasi-equal}
With the notation of Definition \ref{definition-serre-quasi-equal}, let
$$
Q : \mathcal{A} \longrightarrow \mathcal{A}/\mathcal{C}
$$
be the quotient functor.
\begin{enumerate}
\item A subobject $A' \subset A$ is quasi-equal to $A$ if and only if
$Q(A') \to Q(A)$ is an isomorphism. A quotient $B \to B/N$ is quasi-equal
to $B$ if and only if $Q(B) \to Q(B/N)$ is an isomorphism.
\item If $A' \subset A'' \subset A$ and $A'$ is quasi-equal to $A$, then
$A''$ is quasi-equal to $A$.
\item If $A_1, A_2 \subset A$ are quasi-equal to $A$, then
$A_1 \cap A_2$ is quasi-equal to $A$.
\item If $N' \subset N \subset B$ and $B/N$ is quasi-equal to $B$, then
$B/N'$ is quasi-equal to $B$. If $B/N_1$ and $B/N_2$ are quasi-equal to
$B$, then so is $B/(N_1 + N_2)$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The functor $Q$ is exact and has kernel $\mathcal{C}$ by
Lemma \ref{lemma-serre-subcategory-is-kernel}. Hence the image under $Q$
of $A' \to A$ is an isomorphism if and only if $Q(A/A') = 0$, which is
equivalent to $A/A' \in \mathcal{C}$. The quotient case is dual. This
proves (1).

For (2), the object $A/A''$ is a quotient of $A/A'$, and a Serre
subcategory is closed under quotients.

For (3), the canonical morphism
$$
A/(A_1 \cap A_2) \longrightarrow A/A_1 \oplus A/A_2
$$
is injective. Its target belongs to $\mathcal{C}$, and a Serre subcategory
is closed under subobjects. Thus $A/(A_1 \cap A_2)$ belongs to
$\mathcal{C}$.

For (4), if $N' \subset N$ and $N \in \mathcal{C}$, then
$N' \in \mathcal{C}$. Moreover, $N_1 + N_2$ is a quotient of
$N_1 \oplus N_2$, so it belongs to $\mathcal{C}$ whenever $N_1$ and
$N_2$ do.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma-serre-quotient-morphism-representatives}
\begin{reference}
\cite[Chapter I, \S 1.11, p. 138]{Tohoku}
\end{reference}
Let $\mathcal{A}$ be an abelian category, let
$\mathcal{C} \subset \mathcal{A}$ be a Serre subcategory, and write
$$
Q : \mathcal{A} \longrightarrow \mathcal{A}/\mathcal{C}
$$
for the quotient functor. Let $A, B$ be objects of $\mathcal{A}$. Every
morphism
$$
\alpha : Q(A) \longrightarrow Q(B)
$$
can be represented by a morphism
$$
f : A' \longrightarrow B/N
$$
where $A' \subset A$ is quasi-equal to $A$ and $B/N$ is quasi-equal to
$B$. More precisely, if $j : A' \to A$ and $q : B \to B/N$ are the
canonical maps, then
$$
\alpha = Q(q)^{-1} \circ Q(f) \circ Q(j)^{-1}.
$$

Two representatives
$$
f_i : A_i \longrightarrow B/N_i, \qquad i = 1, 2,
$$
define the same morphism if and only if there exist
\begin{enumerate}
\item a subobject $A_0 \subset A_1 \cap A_2$ quasi-equal to $A$, and
\item a subobject $N \subset B$ belonging to $\mathcal{C}$ and containing
$N_1 + N_2$,
\end{enumerate}
such that the two induced morphisms
$$
A_0 \longrightarrow B/N
$$
are equal.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let
$$
S = \{s \in \text{Arrows}(\mathcal{A}) \mid
\Ker(s), \Coker(s) \in \mathcal{C}\}.
$$
By the construction in Lemma
\ref{lemma-serre-subcategory-is-kernel} and Categories, Lemma
\ref{categories-lemma-morphisms-right-localization}, we may write
$$
\alpha = Q(f) \circ Q(s)^{-1}
$$
for morphisms $s : X \to A$ in $S$ and $f : X \to B$.

Put $K = \Ker(s)$ and factor $s$ as
$$
X \xrightarrow{e} A' \xrightarrow{j} A,
\qquad A' = \Im(s).
$$
Since $\Coker(j) = \Coker(s)$ belongs to $\mathcal{C}$, the subobject
$A' \subset A$ is quasi-equal to $A$. Let
$$
N = \Im(K \longrightarrow X \xrightarrow{f} B).
$$
The object $N$ belongs to $\mathcal{C}$ because it is a quotient of
$K \in \mathcal{C}$. If $q : B \to B/N$ is the quotient map, then
$qf$ vanishes on $K$ and therefore descends uniquely to a morphism
$f' : A' \to B/N$ such that
$$
f'e = qf.
$$
The morphisms $Q(e)$, $Q(j)$, and $Q(q)$ are isomorphisms. Since
$s = je$, we obtain
\begin{align*}
Q(q)^{-1}Q(f')Q(j)^{-1}
&= Q(q)^{-1}Q(f')Q(e)Q(s)^{-1} \\
&= Q(q)^{-1}Q(q)Q(f)Q(s)^{-1} \\
&= \alpha.
\end{align*}
This proves existence.

Now let $f_i : A_i \to B/N_i$ be two representatives. Put
$$
D = A_1 \cap A_2, \qquad N_0 = N_1 + N_2, \qquad B_0 = B/N_0.
$$
By Lemma \ref{lemma-serre-quasi-equal}, $D$ is quasi-equal to $A$ and
$B_0$ is quasi-equal to $B$. Restricting both representatives to $D$
and mapping their targets to $B_0$ gives morphisms
$$
k_1, k_2 : D \longrightarrow B_0.
$$
If the representatives define the same morphism, then
$Q(k_1) = Q(k_2)$. For $d = k_1 - k_2$, exactness of $Q$ gives
$$
Q(\Im(d)) = \Im(Q(d)) = 0.
$$
Thus $\Im(d)$ belongs to $\mathcal{C}$. Quotienting $B_0$ by
$\Im(d)$ makes $k_1$ and $k_2$ equal. Writing this quotient as $B/N$,
the kernel $N$ of the composite $B \to B_0 \to B_0/\Im(d)$ belongs to
$\mathcal{C}$ because it is an extension of $N_0$ by $\Im(d)$. Taking
$A_0 = D$ proves the required condition.

Conversely, suppose $A_0$ and $N$ have the stated properties. Let
$j_0 : A_0 \to A$ and $q_0 : B \to B/N$ be the canonical maps. Both
become isomorphisms under $Q$. If $k_i : A_0 \to B/N$ are the two
induced maps, then
$$
Q(q_0) \circ \alpha_i \circ Q(j_0) = Q(k_i).
$$
Hence $k_1 = k_2$ implies $\alpha_1 = \alpha_2$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma-serre-quotient-morphisms-colimit}
\begin{reference}
\cite[Chapter I, \S 1.11, p. 138]{Tohoku}
\end{reference}
Let $\mathcal{A}$ be an abelian category and let
$\mathcal{C} \subset \mathcal{A}$ be a Serre subcategory. Assume that the
subobjects of every object of $\mathcal{A}$ form a set. Let $A, B$ be
objects of $\mathcal{A}$.

Let $I(A, B)$ be the set of pairs $(A', N)$, where $A' \subset A$ is
quasi-equal to $A$ and $N \subset B$ is an object of $\mathcal{C}$, with
the ordering
$$
(A', N) \leq (A'', N') \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
A'' \subset A' \text{ and } N \subset N'.
$$
Then $I(A, B)$ is directed. Restriction and passage to a quotient make
the groups $\Hom_\mathcal{A}(A', B/N)$ into a system over $I(A, B)$, and
there is a canonical isomorphism of abelian groups
$$
\colim_{(A', N) \in I(A, B)} \Hom_\mathcal{A}(A', B/N)
\longrightarrow
\Hom_{\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{C}}(Q(A), Q(B)),
$$
where $Q : \mathcal{A} \to \mathcal{A}/\mathcal{C}$ is the quotient
functor.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The set $I(A, B)$ is nonempty because it contains $(A, 0)$. Given two
elements $(A_1, N_1)$ and $(A_2, N_2)$, the pair
$$
(A_1 \cap A_2, N_1 + N_2)
$$
is an upper bound by Lemma \ref{lemma-serre-quasi-equal}. Thus $I(A, B)$
is directed. If $(A', N) \leq (A'', N')$, the transition map is
$$
\Hom_\mathcal{A}(A', B/N) \longrightarrow
\Hom_\mathcal{A}(A'', B/N')
$$
obtained by restricting to $A''$ and composing with $B/N \to B/N'$.

For $(A', N) \in I(A, B)$, let $j : A' \to A$ and $q : B \to B/N$
be the canonical maps. The rule
$$
f \longmapsto Q(q)^{-1} \circ Q(f) \circ Q(j)^{-1}
$$
is compatible with the transition maps and gives the displayed
homomorphism. It is surjective by
Lemma \ref{lemma-serre-quotient-morphism-representatives}. The equality
criterion in the same lemma says exactly that two elements have the same
image if and only if their images agree at some common upper bound in
$I(A, B)$. This is the equivalence relation defining the colimit. Hence
the homomorphism is injective as well.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma-serre-quotient-compose-representatives}
\begin{reference}
\cite[Chapter I, \S 1.11, pp. 138--139]{Tohoku}
\end{reference}
Let $\mathcal{A}$ be an abelian category and let
$\mathcal{C} \subset \mathcal{A}$ be a Serre subcategory, and write
$$
Q : \mathcal{A} \longrightarrow \mathcal{A}/\mathcal{C}
$$
for the quotient functor. Suppose that
the morphisms
$$
f : A' \longrightarrow B/N
\quad\text{and}\quad
g : B'' \longrightarrow C/M
$$
represent morphisms $\alpha : Q(A) \to Q(B)$ and
$\beta : Q(B) \to Q(C)$ as in
Lemma \ref{lemma-serre-quotient-morphism-representatives}.

Let $h : B'' \to B/N$ be the canonical morphism and factor it as
$$
B'' \xrightarrow{p} T \xrightarrow{i} B/N,
\qquad T = \Im(h).
$$
Let $A_0 = f^{-1}(T)$ and denote by $u : A_0 \to T$ the induced
morphism. If $K = \Ker(p)$, let
$$
L = \Im(K \longrightarrow B'' \xrightarrow{g} C/M),
$$
and let $M' \subset C$ be the inverse image of $L$ under $C \to C/M$.
Then $A_0$ is quasi-equal to $A$, the quotient $C/M'$ is quasi-equal to
$C$, and $g$ induces a morphism $v : T \to C/M'$. The composite
$$
A_0 \xrightarrow{u} T \xrightarrow{v} C/M'
$$
represents $\beta \circ \alpha$.

In particular, this rule is independent of the chosen representatives
and gives the composition in $\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{C}$. It is bilinear
and associative, and the class of $\text{id}_A$ is the identity of
$Q(A)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The kernel of $h$ is $B'' \cap N$, hence belongs to $\mathcal{C}$. The
cokernel of $h$ is $B/(B'' + N)$, which is a quotient of $B/B''$ and
hence also belongs to $\mathcal{C}$. Thus $T$ is quasi-equal both to the
quotient $B/N$ and to the subobject $B''$ in the corresponding senses.
Since
$$
A'/A_0 \longrightarrow (B/N)/T
$$
is injective, $A'/A_0$ belongs to $\mathcal{C}$. It follows that $A_0$
is quasi-equal to $A$ from the exact sequence
$$
0 \longrightarrow A'/A_0 \longrightarrow A/A_0
\longrightarrow A/A' \longrightarrow 0.
$$

The object $K$ belongs to $\mathcal{C}$, and hence so does its quotient
$L$. There is a short exact sequence
$$
0 \longrightarrow M \longrightarrow M' \longrightarrow L
\longrightarrow 0.
$$
Thus $M'$ belongs to $\mathcal{C}$ and $C/M'$ is quasi-equal to $C$.
If $q' : C/M \to C/M'$ is the quotient map, then $q'g$ vanishes on
$K = \Ker(p)$. It therefore descends uniquely to a morphism
$v : T \to C/M'$ satisfying
$$
vp = q'g.
$$

Let $r : A_0 \to A'$ be the inclusion. By construction,
$$
iu = fr.
$$
Let $j_A : A' \to A$, $j_B : B'' \to B$, $q_N : B \to B/N$, and
$q_M : C \to C/M$ be the canonical maps. Set
$$
j_0 = j_A r : A_0 \longrightarrow A
\quad\text{and}\quad
q_{M'} = q'q_M : C \longrightarrow C/M'.
$$
We have
$$
ip = q_Nj_B.
$$
Since all the relevant maps become isomorphisms under $Q$, we compute
\begin{align*}
Q(q_{M'})^{-1}Q(vu)Q(j_0)^{-1}
&= Q(q_M)^{-1}Q(g)Q(p)^{-1}Q(i)^{-1}
Q(f)Q(j_A)^{-1} \\
&= Q(q_M)^{-1}Q(g)Q(j_B)^{-1}Q(q_N)^{-1}
Q(f)Q(j_A)^{-1} \\
&= \beta \circ \alpha.
\end{align*}
Thus $vu$ is a representative of the composite. Independence,
bilinearity, associativity, and the identity assertion follow because
the displayed representative maps to the existing composition in the
abelian category $\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{C}$ constructed in
Lemma \ref{lemma-serre-subcategory-is-kernel}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma-quotient-by-kernel-exact-functor}
Let $\mathcal{A}$, $\mathcal{B}$ be abelian categories.
Let $F : \mathcal{A} \to \mathcal{B}$ be an exact functor.
Let $\mathcal{C} \subset \mathcal{A}$ be a Serre subcategory
contained in the kernel of $F$.
Then $\mathcal{C} = \Ker(F)$ if and only if the induced functor
$\overline{F} : \mathcal{A}/\mathcal{C} \to \mathcal{B}$
(Lemma \ref{lemma-serre-subcategory-is-kernel}) is faithful.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
We will use the results of Lemma \ref{lemma-serre-subcategory-is-kernel}
without further mention.
The ``only if'' direction is true because the kernel of $\overline{F}$ is zero
by construction. Namely, if $f : X \to Y$ is a morphism in
$\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{C}$ such that $\overline{F}(f) = 0$, then
$\overline{F}(\Im(f)) = \Im(\overline{F}(f)) = 0$, hence $\Im(f) = 0$ by the
assumption on the kernel of $F$. Thus $f = 0$.

\medskip\noindent
For the ``if'' direction, let $X$ be an object of $\mathcal{A}$ such that $F(X)
= 0$. Then $\overline{F}(\text{id}_X) = \text{id}_{\overline{F}(X)} = 0$, thus
$\text{id}_X = 0$ in $\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{C}$ by faithfulness of
$\overline{F}$. Hence $X = 0$ in $\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{C}$, that is $X \in
\Ob(\mathcal{C})$.
\end{proof}






\section{K-groups}
\label{section-K-groups}

\noindent
A tiny bit about $K_0$ of an abelian category.

\begin{definition}
\label{definition-K-zero}
Let $\mathcal{A}$ be an abelian category.
We denote $K_0(\mathcal{A})$ the
{\it zeroth $K$-group of $\mathcal{A}$}.
It is the abelian group constructed as follows.
Take the free abelian group
on the objects of $\mathcal{A}$
and for every short exact sequence
$0 \to A \to B \to C \to 0$
impose the relation $[B] - [A] - [C] = 0$.
\end{definition}

\noindent
Another way to say this is that there is a presentation
$$
\bigoplus_{A \to B \to C\text{ ses}}
\mathbf{Z}[A \to B \to C]
\longrightarrow
\bigoplus_{A \in \Ob(\mathcal{A})}
\mathbf{Z}[A]
\longrightarrow
K_0(\mathcal{A})
\longrightarrow
0
$$
with $[A \to B \to C] \mapsto [B] - [A] - [C]$ of $K_0(\mathcal{A})$.
The short exact sequence $0 \to 0 \to 0 \to 0 \to 0$
leads to the relation $[0] = 0$ in $K_0(\mathcal{A})$.
There are no set-theoretical issues as all of our categories
are ``small'' if not mentioned otherwise.
Some examples of $K$-groups for categories of modules
over rings where computed in
Algebra, Section \ref{algebra-section-K-groups}.

\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma-exact-functor-K-groups}
Let $F : \mathcal{A} \to \mathcal{B}$ be an exact functor between
abelian categories. Then $F$ induces a homomorphism of $K$-groups
$K_0(F) : K_0(\mathcal{A}) \to K_0(\mathcal{B})$ by simply setting
$K_0(F)([A]) = [F(A)]$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Proves itself.
\end{proof}

\noindent
Suppose we are given an object $M$ of an abelian category $\mathcal{A}$
and a complex of the form
\begin{equation}
\label{equation-cyclic-complex}
\xymatrix{
\ldots \ar[r] &
M \ar[r]^\varphi &
M \ar[r]^\psi &
M \ar[r]^\varphi &
M \ar[r] & \ldots
}
\end{equation}
In this situation we define
$$
H^0(M, \varphi, \psi) = \Ker(\psi)/\Im(\varphi)
, \quad\text{and}\quad
H^1(M, \varphi, \psi) = \Ker(\varphi)/\Im(\psi).
$$

\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma-serre-subcategory-K-groups}
Let $\mathcal{A}$ be an abelian category.
Let $\mathcal{C} \subset \mathcal{A}$ be a Serre subcategory and
set $\mathcal{B} = \mathcal{A}/\mathcal{C}$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item The exact functors $\mathcal{C} \to \mathcal{A}$ and
$\mathcal{A} \to \mathcal{B}$ induce an exact sequence
$$
K_0(\mathcal{C}) \to
K_0(\mathcal{A}) \to
K_0(\mathcal{B}) \to
0
$$
of $K$-groups, and
\item the kernel of $K_0(\mathcal{C}) \to K_0(\mathcal{A})$ is equal
to the collection of elements of the form
$$
[H^0(M, \varphi, \psi)] - [H^1(M, \varphi, \psi)]
$$
where $(M, \varphi, \psi)$ is a complex as in (\ref{equation-cyclic-complex})
with the property that it becomes exact in $\mathcal{B}$; in other words
that $H^0(M, \varphi, \psi)$ and $H^1(M, \varphi, \psi)$ are
objects of $\mathcal{C}$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Proof of (1). It is clear that $K_0(\mathcal{A}) \to K_0(\mathcal{B})$
is surjective and that the composition $K_0(\mathcal{C}) \to
K_0(\mathcal{A}) \to K_0(\mathcal{B})$ is zero. Let $x \in K_0(\mathcal{A})$
be an element mapping to zero in $K_0(\mathcal{B})$. We can write
$x = [A] - [A']$ with $A, A'$ in $\mathcal{A}$ (fun exercise).
Denote $B, B'$ the corresponding objects of $\mathcal{B}$. The fact that
$x$ maps to zero in $K_0(\mathcal{B})$
means that there exists a finite set $I = I^+ \amalg I^{-}$,
for each $i \in I$ a short exact sequence
$$
0 \to B_i \to B'_i \to B''_i \to 0
$$
in $\mathcal{B}$ such that we have
$$
[B] - [B'] = \sum\nolimits_{i \in I^{+}} ([B'_i] - [B_i] - [B''_i])
-
\sum\nolimits_{i \in I^{-}} ([B'_i] - [B_i] - [B''_i])
$$
in the free abelian group on isomorphism classes of objects of $\mathcal{B}$.
We can rewrite this as
$$
[B]
+ \sum\nolimits_{i \in I^{+}} ([B_i] + [B''_i])
+ \sum\nolimits_{i \in I^{-}} [B'_i]
=
[B']
+ \sum\nolimits_{i \in I^{-}} ([B_i] + [B''_i])
+ \sum\nolimits_{i \in I^{+}} [B'_i].
$$
Since the right and left hand side should contain the same isomorphism classes
of objects of $\mathcal{B}$ counted with multiplicity, this means there should
be a bijection
$$
\tau :
\{B\} \amalg \{B_i, B''_i; i \in I^+\} \amalg \{B'_i; i \in I^-\}
\longrightarrow
\{B'\} \amalg \{B_i, B''_i; i \in I^-\} \amalg \{B'_i; i \in I^+\}
$$
such that $N$ and $\tau(N)$ are isomorphic in $\mathcal{B}$.
The proof of Lemmas \ref{lemma-serre-subcategory-is-kernel} and
\ref{lemma-localization-abelian} show that we choose for $i \in I$
a short exact sequence
$$
0 \to A_i \to A'_i \to A''_i \to 0
$$
in $\mathcal{A}$ such that $B_i, B'_i, B''_i$ are isomorphic to the
images of $A_i, A'_i, A''_i$ in $\mathcal{B}$. This implies that the
corresponding bijection
$$
\tau :
\{A\} \amalg \{A_i, A''_i; i \in I^+\} \amalg \{A'_i; i \in I^-\}
\longrightarrow
\{A'\} \amalg \{A_i, A''_i; i \in I^-\} \amalg \{A'_i; i \in I^+\}
$$
satisfies the property that $M$ and $\tau(M)$ are objects of $\mathcal{A}$
which become isomorphic in $\mathcal{B}$. This means $[M] - [\tau(M)]$
is in the image of $K_0(\mathcal{C}) \to K_0(\mathcal{A})$. Namely,
the isomorphism in $\mathcal{B}$ is given by a diagram
$M \leftarrow M' \rightarrow \tau(M)$ in $\mathcal{A}$ where both
$M' \to M$ and $M' \to \tau(M)$ have kernel and cokernel in $\mathcal{C}$.
Working backwards we conclude that $x = [A] - [A']$ is in the image
of $K_0(\mathcal{C}) \to K_0(\mathcal{A})$ and the proof of part (1)
is complete.

\medskip\noindent
Proof of (2). The proof is similar to the proof of (1) but slightly
more bookkeeping is involved. First we remark that any class of the type
$[H^0(M, \varphi, \psi)] - [H^1(M, \varphi, \psi)]$ is zero
in $K_0(\mathcal{A})$ by the following calculation
\begin{align*}
0 & = [M] - [M] \\
& =  [\Ker(\varphi)] + [\Im(\varphi)]
- [\Ker(\psi)] - [\Im(\psi)] \\
& =
[\Ker(\varphi)/\Im(\psi)] -
[\Ker(\psi)/\Im(\varphi)] \\
& = [H^1(M, \varphi, \psi)] - [H^0(M, \varphi, \psi)]
\end{align*}
as desired. Hence it suffices to show that any element in the kernel
of $K_0(\mathcal{C}) \to K_0(\mathcal{A})$ is of this form.

\medskip\noindent
Any element $x$ in $K_0(\mathcal{C})$ can be represented as the
difference $x = [P] - [Q]$ of two objects of $\mathcal{C}$ (fun exercise).
Suppose that this element maps to zero in $K_0(\mathcal{A})$.
This means that there exist
\begin{enumerate}
\item a finite set $I = I^{+} \amalg I^{-}$,
\item for $i \in I$ a short exact sequence $0 \to A_i \to B_i \to C_i \to 0$
in $\mathcal{A}$
\end{enumerate}
such that
$$
[P] - [Q] =
\sum\nolimits_{i \in I^{+}} ([B_i] - [A_i] - [C_i])
-
\sum\nolimits_{i \in I^{-}} ([B_i] - [A_i] - [C_i])
$$
in the free abelian group on the objects of $\mathcal{A}$.
We can rewrite this as
$$
[P]
+ \sum\nolimits_{i \in I^{+}} ([A_i] + [C_i])
+ \sum\nolimits_{i \in I^{-}} [B_i]
=
[Q]
+ \sum\nolimits_{i \in I^{-}} ([A_i] + [C_i])
+ \sum\nolimits_{i \in I^{+}} [B_i].
$$
Since the right and left hand side should contain the same objects
of $\mathcal{A}$ counted with multiplicity, this means there should be
a bijection $\tau$ between the terms which occur above. Set
$$
T^{+} =
\{p\}\ \amalg\ \{a, c\} \times I^{+}\ \amalg\ \{b\} \times I^{-}
$$
and
$$
T^{-} =
\{q\}\ \amalg\ \{a, c\} \times I^{-}\ \amalg\ \{b\} \times I^{+}.
$$
Set $T = T^{+} \amalg T^{-} = \{p, q\} \amalg \{a, b, c\} \times I$.
For $t \in T$ define
$$
O(t)
=
\left\{
\begin{matrix}
P & \text{if} & t = p \\
Q & \text{if} & t = q \\
A_i & \text{if} & t = (a, i) \\
B_i & \text{if} & t = (b, i) \\
C_i & \text{if} & t = (c, i)
\end{matrix}
\right.
$$
Hence we can view $\tau : T^{+} \to T^{-}$ as a bijection
such that $O(t) = O(\tau(t))$ for all $t \in T^{+}$.
Let $t^{-}_0 = \tau(p)$ and let $t^{+}_0 \in T^{+}$ be the
unique element such that $\tau(t^{+}_0) = q$.
Consider the object
$$
M^{+} = \bigoplus\nolimits_{t \in T^{+}} O(t)
$$
By using $\tau$ we see that it is equal to the object
$$
M^{-} = \bigoplus\nolimits_{t \in T^{-}} O(t)
$$
Consider the map
$$
\varphi : M^{+} \longrightarrow M^{-}
$$
which on the summand $O(t) = A_i$ corresponding to $t = (a, i)$, $i \in I^{+}$
uses the map $A_i \to B_i$ into the summand $O((b, i)) = B_i$ of $M^{-}$
and on the summand $O(t) = B_i$ corresponding to $(b, i)$, $i \in I^{-}$
uses the map $B_i \to C_i$ into the summand $O((c, i)) = C_i$ of $M^{-}$.
The map is zero on the summands corresponding to $p$
and $(c, i)$, $i \in I^{+}$.
Similarly, consider the map
$$
\psi : M^{-} \longrightarrow M^{+}
$$
which on the summand $O(t) = A_i$ corresponding to $t = (a, i)$, $i \in I^{-}$
uses the map $A_i \to B_i$ into the summand $O((b, i)) = B_i$ of $M^{+}$
and on the summand $O(t) = B_i$ corresponding to $(b, i)$, $i \in I^{+}$
uses the map $B_i \to C_i$ into the summand $O((c, i)) = C_i$ of $M^{+}$.
The map is zero on the summands corresponding to $q$ and
$(c, i)$, $i \in I^{-}$.

\medskip\noindent
Note that the kernel of $\varphi$ is equal to the direct sum of the
summand $P$ and the summands $O((c, i)) = C_i$, $i \in I^{+}$ and
the subobjects $A_i$ inside the summands $O((b, i)) = B_i$, $i \in I^{-}$.
The image of $\psi$ is equal to the direct sum of the
summands $O((c, i)) = C_i$, $i \in I^{+}$ and
the subobjects $A_i$ inside the summands $O((b, i)) = B_i$, $i \in I^{-}$.
In other words we see that
$$
P \cong \Ker(\varphi)/\Im(\psi).
$$
In exactly the same way we see that
$$
Q \cong \Ker(\psi)/\Im(\varphi).
$$
Since as we remarked above the existence of the bijection
$\tau$ shows that $M^{+} = M^{-}$ we see that the lemma follows.
\end{proof}






\section{Cohomological delta-functors}
\label{section-cohomological-delta-functor}

\begin{definition}
\label{definition-cohomological-delta-functor}
Let $\mathcal{A}, \mathcal{B}$ be abelian categories.
A {\it cohomological $\delta$-functor} or simply a
{\it $\delta$-functor} from $\mathcal{A}$
to $\mathcal{B}$ is given by the following data:
\begin{enumerate}
\item a collection $F^n : \mathcal{A} \to \mathcal{B}$, $n \geq 0$ of additive
functors, and
\item for every short exact sequence $0 \to A \to B \to C \to 0$
of $\mathcal{A}$
a collection $\delta_{A \to B \to C} : F^n(C) \to F^{n + 1}(A)$, $n \geq 0$
of morphisms of $\mathcal{B}$.
\end{enumerate}
These data are assumed to satisfy the following axioms
\begin{enumerate}
\item for every short exact sequence as above the sequence
$$
\xymatrix{
0 \ar[r] &
F^0(A) \ar[r] &
F^0(B) \ar[r] &
F^0(C) \ar[lld]^{\delta_{A \to B \to C}} \\
 &
F^1(A) \ar[r] &
F^1(B) \ar[r] &
F^1(C) \ar[lld]^{\delta_{A \to B \to C}} \\
 &
F^2(A) \ar[r] &
F^2(B) \ar[r] &
\ldots
}
$$
is exact, and
\item for every morphism $(A \to B \to C) \to (A' \to B' \to C')$
of short exact sequences of $\mathcal{A}$ the diagrams
$$
\xymatrix{
F^n(C) \ar[d] \ar[rr]_{\delta_{A \to B \to C}} & & F^{n + 1}(A) \ar[d] \\
F^n(C') \ar[rr]^{\delta_{A' \to B' \to C'}} & & F^{n + 1}(A')
}
$$
are commutative.
\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\noindent
Note that this in particular implies that $F^0$ is left exact.

\begin{definition}[Graded connecting functors]
\label{definition-graded-connecting-functor}
\begin{reference}
\cite[Chapter II, \S 2.1, pp. 139--140]{Tohoku}
\end{reference}
Let $\mathcal A$ be an abelian category and let $\mathcal B$ be an
additive category. Let
$$
a,b\in\mathbf Z\cup\{-\infty,+\infty\},\qquad a+1<b,
$$
put
$$
I=\{i\in\mathbf Z\mid a<i<b\},
$$
and fix $\epsilon\in\{-1,1\}$.

A covariant graded connecting functor of step $\epsilon$ on $I$ consists
of additive functors
$$
T^i:\mathcal A\longrightarrow\mathcal B,\qquad i\in I,
$$
and, for every short exact sequence
$$
0\longrightarrow A'\longrightarrow A\longrightarrow A''
\longrightarrow 0
$$
and every $i$ such that $i,i+\epsilon\in I$, a boundary morphism
$$
\partial^i:T^i(A'')\longrightarrow T^{i+\epsilon}(A').
$$
The boundary morphisms are required to be natural in morphisms of short
exact sequences, and the string
$$
\cdots\longrightarrow T^i(A')\longrightarrow T^i(A)
\longrightarrow T^i(A'')
\xrightarrow{\partial^i}T^{i+\epsilon}(A')
\longrightarrow\cdots
$$
is required to be a complex.

A contravariant graded connecting functor of step $\epsilon$ is defined
similarly, with additive functors
$$
T^i:\mathcal A^{opp}\longrightarrow\mathcal B
$$
and boundary morphisms
$$
\partial^i:T^i(A')\longrightarrow T^{i+\epsilon}(A'').
$$
Naturality is contravariant in a morphism of short exact sequences, and
the associated string
$$
\cdots\longrightarrow T^i(A'')\longrightarrow T^i(A)
\longrightarrow T^i(A')
\xrightarrow{\partial^i}T^{i+\epsilon}(A'')
\longrightarrow\cdots
$$
is required to be a complex.

In both displays the indices proceed in the direction of $\epsilon$.
Only terms whose degrees belong to $I$ are retained: at a finite end of
$I$ the string stops, and no zero object is inserted. A graded connecting
functor of step $+1$ is also called a $\partial$-functor, and one of step
$-1$ a $\partial^*$-functor. A $\partial$-functor defined in every
integer degree is also called a connected sequence of functors.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Exact graded connecting functors]
\label{definition-exact-graded-connecting-functor}
\begin{reference}
\cite[Chapter II, \S 2.1, p. 140]{Tohoku}
\end{reference}
In Definition \ref{definition-graded-connecting-functor}, suppose in
addition that $\mathcal B$ is abelian. The graded connecting functor is
called exact if, for every short exact sequence in $\mathcal A$, its
associated string is exact at every displayed term having both an incoming
and an outgoing arrow.

An exact graded connecting functor defined in all integer degrees is
called a cohomological functor when its step is $+1$, and a homological
functor when its step is $-1$.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
\label{remark-one-sided-two-sided-delta-functors}
The convention in Definition
\ref{definition-cohomological-delta-functor} is the one-sided convention
$I=\{i\in\mathbf Z\mid i\geq 0\}$ with step $+1$, together with the
additional initial arrow
$$
0\longrightarrow F^0(A').
$$
Consequently, a cohomological delta-functor in that sense gives an exact
graded connecting functor after the initial zero is forgotten, but the
converse need not be left exact in its lowest degree. The unpadded
finite-interval convention above imposes no exactness condition at an end
term lacking one of its adjacent arrows.
\end{remark}

\begin{lemma}[Dualities for graded connecting functors]
\label{lemma-graded-connecting-functor-dualities}
\begin{reference}
\cite[Chapter II, \S 2.1, p. 140]{Tohoku}
\end{reference}
Let $T$ be a covariant or contravariant graded connecting functor on
$I=\{i\mid a<i<b\}$, of step $\epsilon$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item Put
$$
I^\vee=\{i\in\mathbf Z\mid -b<i<-a\},\qquad S^i=T^{-i}.
$$
With boundary
$$
\partial_S^i=\partial_T^{-i},
$$
the functors $S^i$ have the same variance as the $T^i$ and form a graded
connecting functor of step $-\epsilon$ on $I^\vee$.

\item Taking opposite functors in the target changes the variance and the
step. More precisely, if the $T^i$ are covariant, put
$$
V^i=(T^i)^{opp}:\mathcal A^{opp}\longrightarrow\mathcal B^{opp},
$$
and if the $T^i$ are contravariant, put
$$
V^i=(T^i)^{opp}:\mathcal A\longrightarrow\mathcal B^{opp}.
$$
Then $V$ has step $-\epsilon$, with boundary in degree $i$ given by
$$
\partial_V^i=
\left(\partial_T^{\,i-\epsilon}\right)^{opp}.
$$

\item A contravariant graded connecting functor on $\mathcal A$ is the same
thing as a covariant graded connecting functor of the same step on
$\mathcal A^{opp}$: the short exact sequence
$$
0\longrightarrow A'\longrightarrow A\longrightarrow A''\longrightarrow 0
$$
is replaced by
$$
0\longrightarrow A''\longrightarrow A\longrightarrow A'\longrightarrow 0
$$
in $\mathcal A^{opp}$.
\end{enumerate}
These operations preserve the complex condition. When $\mathcal B$ is
abelian, they also preserve exactness. In particular, by combining degree
reversal with passage to the opposite source when necessary, each of the
four variance-and-step conventions reduces to the covariant
$\partial$-functor convention.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Degree reversal merely relabels the associated string. For the second
construction, the associated string in $\mathcal B^{opp}$ is the opposite
of the original string with its order reversed; the index shift in the
boundary is forced by
$$
\left(\partial_T^{\,i-\epsilon}\right)^{opp}.
$$
For the third construction the two displayed associated strings are
literally the same string after the short exact sequence is reversed.
The assertions about complexes and exactness follow.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma-dualize-delta-functor}
\begin{reference}
\cite[Chapter III, \S 4, no. 70, exact sequences for the functors
$B^q$ and $T^q$, p. 264]{FAC}
\end{reference}
\begin{history}
In the cited source, $F^q=B^q$ is the cohomological delta functor attached
to the positive graded Koszul--{\v C}ech complexes and $D$ is the exact
degreewise $K$-dual of Algebra, Definition
\ref{algebra-definition-graded-K-dual}. Thus $T^q=D\circ B^q$ is
contravariant and its displayed exact sequence is the reverse of the
$B^q$ sequence. The statement below isolates that categorical operation.
\end{history}
Let $\mathcal A$, $\mathcal B$, and $\mathcal C$ be abelian categories.
Let $(F^n,\delta)$ be a cohomological delta functor from $\mathcal A$ to
$\mathcal B$, and let
$D:\mathcal B^{opp}\to\mathcal C$ be an additive exact functor. Set
$T^n=D\circ F^n$. Then the $T^n$ are contravariant functors and every short
exact sequence $0\to A\to B\to C\to0$ in $\mathcal A$ gives an exact
sequence
$$
\ldots\longrightarrow T^{n+1}(A)\longrightarrow T^n(C)
\longrightarrow T^n(B)\longrightarrow T^n(A)\longrightarrow\ldots.
$$
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The delta-functor axioms give an exact sequence
$$
\ldots\longrightarrow F^n(A)\longrightarrow F^n(B)
\longrightarrow F^n(C)\longrightarrow F^{n+1}(A)\longrightarrow\ldots.
$$
Applying the exact contravariant functor $D$ reverses all arrows and
preserves exactness, which gives the displayed sequence. Functoriality is
the composite of the covariant functoriality of $F^n$ and the
contravariant functoriality of $D$.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Morphisms of graded connecting functors]
\label{definition-morphism-graded-connecting-functors}
\begin{reference}
\cite[Chapter II, \S 2.1, p. 140]{Tohoku}
\end{reference}
Let $\mathcal A$ be an abelian category and let $\mathcal B$ be an
additive category. Fix an interval $I$ as in Definition
\ref{definition-graded-connecting-functor}, one of the two variances, and
a step $\epsilon \in \{-1,1\}$. Let
$$
T=(T^i,\partial_T^i)
\quad\text{and}\quad
T'=(T'^i,\partial_{T'}^i)
$$
be graded connecting functors with these same fixed data.

A morphism $f:T\longrightarrow T'$ is a collection of natural
transformations
$$
f^i:T^i\longrightarrow T'^i,\qquad i\in I,
$$
compatible with the boundary morphisms as follows. In the covariant case,
for every short exact sequence
$$
0\longrightarrow A'\longrightarrow A\longrightarrow A''
\longrightarrow 0
$$
and every $i$ such that $i,i+\epsilon\in I$, the diagram
$$
\xymatrix{
T^i(A'') \ar[r]^{\partial_T^i} \ar[d]_{f^i_{A''}} &
T^{i+\epsilon}(A') \ar[d]^{f^{i+\epsilon}_{A'}} \\
T'^i(A'') \ar[r]_{\partial_{T'}^i} &
T'^{i+\epsilon}(A')
}
$$
commutes. In the contravariant case the required diagram is
$$
\xymatrix{
T^i(A') \ar[r]^{\partial_T^i} \ar[d]_{f^i_{A'}} &
T^{i+\epsilon}(A'') \ar[d]^{f^{i+\epsilon}_{A''}} \\
T'^i(A') \ar[r]_{\partial_{T'}^i} &
T'^{i+\epsilon}(A'').
}
$$
These conditions apply for both steps: when $\epsilon=-1$, the degree on
the right is $i-1$. Identities, composites, zero morphisms, and sums of
parallel morphisms are defined componentwise, and the displayed
compatibilities are preserved.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Universal graded connecting functors]
\label{definition-universal-graded-connecting-functor}
\begin{reference}
\cite[Chapter II, \S 2.2, p. 140]{Tohoku}
\end{reference}
\begin{history}
The five unbound occurrences of $i_0$ in the printed definition are read
as degree $0$, as required by the immediately following assertion that
the functor is prescribed in degree zero.
\end{history}
Let $\mathcal A$ be an abelian category and let $\mathcal B$ be an
additive category. Fix an interval $I$ as in Definition
\ref{definition-graded-connecting-functor} such that $0\in I$, one of the
two variances, and a step $\epsilon\in\{-1,1\}$. Let $T$ be a graded
connecting functor with these fixed data.

We call $T$ universal if the following condition holds for every graded
connecting functor $T'$ with the same interval, variance, and step.
\begin{enumerate}
\item If $\epsilon=1$, every natural transformation
$$
f^0:T^0\longrightarrow T'^0
$$
extends to a unique morphism of graded connecting functors
$$
f:T\longrightarrow T'
$$
whose component in degree zero is $f^0$.
\item If $\epsilon=-1$, every natural transformation
$$
f^0:T'^0\longrightarrow T^0
$$
extends to a unique morphism of graded connecting functors
$$
f:T'\longrightarrow T
$$
whose component in degree zero is $f^0$.
\end{enumerate}
The interval $I$ may be finite. Neither $T$ nor the competitors $T'$ are
required to be exact.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Uniqueness of universal graded connecting functors]
\label{lemma-uniqueness-universal-graded-connecting-functor}
\begin{reference}
\cite[Chapter II, \S 2.2, p. 140]{Tohoku}
\end{reference}
Fix the categories, interval containing $0$, variance, and step as in
Definition \ref{definition-universal-graded-connecting-functor}. Let $F$
be an additive functor of the fixed variance. Let $T$ and $U$ be
universal graded connecting functors with fixed identifications
$$
\alpha:T^0\xrightarrow{\sim}F
\quad\text{and}\quad
\beta:U^0\xrightarrow{\sim}F.
$$
There exists a unique isomorphism
$$
f:T\longrightarrow U
$$
of graded connecting functors whose degree-zero component is
$\beta^{-1}\circ\alpha$. In particular, if $T^0=F=U^0$, this is the
unique isomorphism whose degree-zero component is $\mathrm{id}_F$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Put $c=\beta^{-1}\circ\alpha$. If $\epsilon=1$, universality of $T$
applied to $c$ gives a morphism $f:T\to U$, and universality of $U$
applied to $c^{-1}$ gives a morphism $g:U\to T$. If $\epsilon=-1$, the
same morphisms are obtained in the opposite order: universality of $U$
applied to $c$ gives $f:T\to U$, and universality of $T$ applied to
$c^{-1}$ gives $g:U\to T$.

In either case, the degree-zero components of $g\circ f$ and
$f\circ g$ are identities. The relevant uniqueness clauses for $T$ and
$U$ therefore give
$$
g\circ f=\mathrm{id}_T
\quad\text{and}\quad
f\circ g=\mathrm{id}_U.
$$
Thus $f$ is an isomorphism. The same uniqueness clause shows that any
morphism $T\to U$ with degree-zero component $c$ equals $f$.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Right and left satellite functors]
\label{definition-right-left-satellite-functors}
\begin{reference}
\cite[Chapter II, \S 2.2, p. 140]{Tohoku}
\end{reference}
\begin{history}
The printed sentence reads ``if $i<0$, put $S_iF=S^{-i}F$.'' This cannot
be the stated left-satellite convention: the subscripted $S_iF$ are
components in nonnegative degrees of a universal $\partial^*$-functor,
whereas for $i<0$ the term $S^{-i}F$ is a positive-degree right
satellite. We use the registered corrected reading $i>0$, equivalently
$S^{-i}F=S_iF$ for $i>0$.
\end{history}
Let $\mathcal A$ be an abelian category and let $\mathcal B$ be an
additive category. Let $F$ be an additive functor of one fixed variance,
from $\mathcal A$ or $\mathcal A^{opp}$ to $\mathcal B$. For an integer
$a>0$, put
$$
I_a=\{0,\ldots,a\}.
$$

A \emph{right satellite system of length $a$ for $F$} is a universal
graded connecting functor $T$ on $I_a$, of the same variance as $F$ and
of step $+1$, together with a fixed identification
$$
\alpha:T^0\xrightarrow{\sim}F.
$$
For a chosen such system, its component $T^n$, $0\leq n\leq a$, is called
the $n$th \emph{right satellite functor} of $F$ and is denoted $S^nF$.

A \emph{left satellite system of length $a$ for $F$} is defined in the
same way using step $-1$. Its component in degree $n$ is called the $n$th
\emph{left satellite functor} and is denoted $S_nF$. For $n>0$ we also
use the signed notation
$$
S^{-n}F:=S_nF.
$$
These notations are used only when a corresponding universal system
exists through degree $n$; this definition makes no existence or
exactness assertion.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Independence of the truncation length]
\label{lemma-satellite-functors-independent-truncation}
\begin{reference}
\cite[Chapter II, \S 2.2, p. 140]{Tohoku}
\end{reference}
Let $(T,\alpha)$ be a right or left satellite system of length $a$ for
$F$. If $0<m\leq a$, then the restriction $T|_{I_m}$, with its given
degree-zero identification, is a satellite system of the same kind and
of length $m$.

Consequently, if satellite systems of the same kind and of lengths
$a,b\geq n$ exist, their components in degree $n$ are canonically
isomorphic. These canonical isomorphisms are compatible with passage
between any three choices. Thus $S^nF$ and $S_nF$, when defined, are
independent of the truncation length up to coherent canonical
isomorphism.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Restriction plainly preserves the graded connecting-functor data. Let
$G$ be any graded connecting functor on $I_m$ with the same variance and
step as $T$. Define its zero extension $\widetilde G$ to $I_a$ by
$$
\widetilde G^j=
\begin{cases}
G^j & \text{if }0\leq j\leq m,\\
0 & \text{if }m<j\leq a,
\end{cases}
$$
using the boundary morphisms of $G$ when both degrees lie in $I_m$ and
the zero morphism for every remaining boundary. This is a graded
connecting functor: naturality is immediate, and every new pair of
consecutive arrows contains a zero morphism. No exactness is being
asserted.

Suppose first that $T$ has step $+1$. Every morphism
$$
T|_{I_m}\longrightarrow G
$$
extends uniquely to a morphism $T\to\widetilde G$ by taking all
components above $m$ to be the unique maps to the zero functor.
Conversely, every morphism $T\to\widetilde G$ restricts to one
$T|_{I_m}\to G$. The only new boundary square meeting degree $m$ has
zero bottom boundary and zero right vertical map, so these two
operations are inverse and preserve the component in degree zero.
Universality of $T$ therefore gives existence and uniqueness of the
required morphism from $T|_{I_m}$ to every competitor $G$.

Suppose instead that $T$ has step $-1$. Every morphism
$$
G\longrightarrow T|_{I_m}
$$
extends uniquely to a morphism $\widetilde G\to T$ by taking all
components above $m$ to be the unique maps from the zero functor. The
only new boundary square meeting degree $m$ has zero top boundary and
zero left vertical map. Restriction and zero extension are again inverse
and preserve degree zero. The terminal form of universality of $T$
therefore proves that $T|_{I_m}$ is universal of step $-1$.

Now let $(T,\alpha)$ and $(U,\beta)$ be systems of the same kind and of
lengths $a,b\geq n$. If $n>0$, restrict both systems to $I_n$ and apply
Lemma \ref{lemma-uniqueness-universal-graded-connecting-functor}. It gives
the unique isomorphism whose degree-zero component is
$\beta^{-1}\circ\alpha$; its degree-$n$ component is the claimed
canonical isomorphism. For $n=0$ use $\beta^{-1}\circ\alpha$ directly.
For a third system $(V,\gamma)$, the composite of the comparisons from
$T$ to $U$ and from $U$ to $V$ has degree-zero component
$$
(\gamma^{-1}\circ\beta)\circ(\beta^{-1}\circ\alpha)
=\gamma^{-1}\circ\alpha.
$$
The same uniqueness lemma identifies it with the direct comparison from
$T$ to $V$, proving coherence.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}
\label{definition-morphism-delta-functors}
Let $\mathcal{A}, \mathcal{B}$ be abelian categories.
Let $(F^n, \delta_F)$ and $(G^n, \delta_G)$ be $\delta$-functors
from $\mathcal{A}$ to $\mathcal{B}$. A {\it morphism of $\delta$-functors
from $F$ to $G$} is a collection of
transformation of functors $t^n : F^n \to G^n$, $n \geq 0$ such
that for every short exact sequence $0 \to A \to B \to C \to 0$
of $\mathcal{A}$ the diagrams
$$
\xymatrix{
F^n(C) \ar[d]_{t^n} \ar[rr]_{\delta_{F, A \to B \to C}} &
& F^{n + 1}(A) \ar[d]^{t^{n + 1}} \\
G^n(C) \ar[rr]^{\delta_{G, A \to B \to C}} & & G^{n + 1}(A)
}
$$
are commutative.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
\label{definition-universal-delta-functor}
Let $\mathcal{A}, \mathcal{B}$ be abelian categories.
Let $F = (F^n, \delta_F)$ be a $\delta$-functor
from $\mathcal{A}$ to $\mathcal{B}$.
We say $F$ is a {\it universal $\delta$-functor} if and only
if for every $\delta$-functor $G = (G^n, \delta_G)$ and any
morphism of functors $t : F^0 \to G^0$ there exists
a unique morphism of $\delta$-functors $\{t^n\}_{n \geq 0} : F \to G$
such that $t = t^0$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
\label{definition-effaceable-functor}
\begin{reference}
\cite[Chapter II, \S 2.2, p. 141]{Tohoku}
\end{reference}
Let $\mathcal{A}, \mathcal{B}$ be abelian categories and let
$F : \mathcal{A} \to \mathcal{B}$ be an additive functor.
\begin{enumerate}
\item We say $F$ is {\it effaceable} if for every object $A$ of
$\mathcal{A}$ there is an injection $u : A \to M$ such that $F(u)=0$.
\item We say $F$ is {\it co-effaceable} if for every object $A$ of
$\mathcal{A}$ there is a surjection $u : P \to A$ such that $F(u)=0$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma-characterize-effaceable-functor}
\begin{reference}
\cite[Chapter II, \S 2.2, p. 141]{Tohoku}
\end{reference}
Let $\mathcal{A}, \mathcal{B}$ be abelian categories and let
$F : \mathcal{A} \to \mathcal{B}$ be an additive functor.
\begin{enumerate}
\item Suppose every object of $\mathcal{A}$ has an injective effacement.
Then $F$ is effaceable if and only if $F(u)=0$ for every injective
effacement $u$.
\item Suppose every object of $\mathcal{A}$ has a projective effacement.
Then $F$ is co-effaceable if and only if $F(u)=0$ for every projective
effacement $u$.
\item Suppose $\mathcal{A}$ has enough injectives. Then $F$ is effaceable
if and only if $F(I)=0$ for every injective object $I$.
\item Suppose $\mathcal{A}$ has enough projectives. Then $F$ is
co-effaceable if and only if $F(P)=0$ for every projective object $P$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Assume $F$ is effaceable and let $i : A \to M$ be an injective effacement.
Choose an injection $u : A \to N$ such that $F(u)=0$. The defining property
of $i$, applied to $u$ and $\text{id}_A$, gives a morphism $h : N \to M$
such that $h \circ u=i$. Hence $F(i)=F(h)\circ F(u)=0$. The converse in
(1) follows by choosing an injective effacement of each object.

The proof of (2) is dual to the proof of (1). Explicitly, if
$p : M \to A$ is a projective effacement and $u : N \to A$ is a
surjection with $F(u)=0$, then the defining property of $p$ gives a
morphism $h : M \to N$ such that $u \circ h=p$. Hence $F(p)=0$.
The converse follows by choosing a projective effacement of each object.

Suppose $\mathcal{A}$ has enough injectives. If $F$ vanishes on injective
objects, then an injection $A \to I$ into an injective object effaces $F$ on
$A$. Conversely, suppose $F$ is effaceable and let $I$ be injective. Choose
an injection $u : I \to M$ such that $F(u)=0$. There is a retraction
$r : M \to I$, and therefore
$$
\text{id}_{F(I)}=F(r)\circ F(u)=0.
$$
Thus $F(I)=0$. This proves (3). The proof of (4) is dual.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[The first right satellite from injective effacements]
\label{lemma-first-right-satellite-injective-effacements}
\begin{reference}
\cite[Chapter II, \S 2.2, Theorem 2.2.2 and its proof,
pp. 141--142]{Tohoku}
\end{reference}
Let $\mathcal A$ and $\mathcal B$ be abelian categories, and suppose every
object of $\mathcal A$ has an injective effacement. For every additive
functor
$$
G:\mathcal A\longrightarrow\mathcal B
$$
there is an additive functor
$$
\Sigma G:\mathcal A\longrightarrow\mathcal B
$$
and boundary morphisms such that:
\begin{enumerate}
\item $(G,\Sigma G)$ is a covariant graded connecting functor of step $+1$
on $\{0,1\}$;
\item with the identity identification in degree zero it is universal;
\item $\Sigma G$ is effaceable.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For every $A$ choose an injective effacement
$$
e_A:A\longrightarrow E_A
$$
and write
$$
\pi_A:E_A\longrightarrow Q_A=\Coker(e_A).
$$
Set
$$
\Sigma G(A)=
\Coker\bigl(G(E_A)\xrightarrow{G(\pi_A)}G(Q_A)\bigr)
$$
and denote the quotient morphism by
$$
\rho_A:G(Q_A)\longrightarrow\Sigma G(A).
$$

Let $f:A\to B$. The defining property of $e_B$, applied to the injection
$e_A$ and the morphism $f$, gives a morphism
$$
\widetilde f:E_A\longrightarrow E_B
\quad\text{such that}\quad
\widetilde f e_A=e_Bf.
$$
It induces a unique morphism
$$
\overline f:Q_A\longrightarrow Q_B
\quad\text{such that}\quad
\overline f\pi_A=\pi_B\widetilde f.
$$
If $\widetilde f'$ is another lift, then
$\widetilde f-\widetilde f'$ kills $e_A$, so there is a morphism
$r:Q_A\to E_B$ such that
$$
\widetilde f-\widetilde f'=r\pi_A.
$$
Since $\pi_A$ is an epimorphism, it follows that
$$
\overline f-\overline f'=\pi_Br.
$$
Consequently,
$$
\rho_BG(\overline f)=\rho_BG(\overline f'),
$$
and hence there is a well-defined morphism $\Sigma G(f)$ characterized by
$$
\Sigma G(f)\rho_A=\rho_BG(\overline f).
$$
The identity of $E_A$ is a lift of $\mathrm{id}_A$, composites of lifts
are lifts of composites, and sums of lifts are lifts of sums. The
independence just proved therefore shows that $\Sigma G$ preserves
identities, composites, and sums. Thus $\Sigma G$ is additive.

Let
$$
\xi:\quad 0\longrightarrow A\xrightarrow{u}B\xrightarrow{p}C
\longrightarrow0
$$
be a short exact sequence. The defining property of $e_A$ gives a morphism
$h:B\to E_A$ such that $hu=e_A$. There is a unique morphism
$\overline h:C\to Q_A$ satisfying
$$
\overline hp=\pi_Ah.
$$
Define
$$
\partial_\xi=
\rho_AG(\overline h):G(C)\longrightarrow\Sigma G(A).
$$
This does not depend on $h$. Indeed, if $h'$ is another choice, then
$h-h'$ kills $u$, so $h-h'=dp$ for some $d:C\to E_A$. Hence
$$
\overline h-\overline h'=\pi_Ad,
$$
which is killed after applying $G$ and then $\rho_A$.

We verify naturality. Let $(a,b,c):\xi\to\xi'$ be a morphism of short exact
sequences, and choose a lift
$$
\widetilde a:E_A\longrightarrow E_{A'}
\quad\text{with}\quad
\widetilde ae_A=e_{A'}a.
$$
Let $h$ and $h'$ be choices used to define the two boundaries. Then
$$
(\widetilde ah-h'b)u=0,
$$
so there is a morphism $d:C\to E_{A'}$ such that
$$
dp=\widetilde ah-h'b.
$$
If $\overline a:Q_A\to Q_{A'}$ is induced by $\widetilde a$, then, after
precomposing with the epimorphism $p$, we obtain
$$
\overline a\,\overline h-\overline h'c=\pi_{A'}d.
$$
Therefore
$$
\Sigma G(a)\partial_\xi
=\partial_{\xi'}G(c),
$$
as required.

The associated string is a complex. We have
$$
G(p)G(u)=0
$$
by functoriality, and
$$
\partial_\xi G(p)
=\rho_AG(\overline hp)
=\rho_AG(\pi_Ah)=0.
$$
Choose a lift $\widetilde u:E_A\to E_B$ used to define $\Sigma G(u)$.
Since
$$
(\widetilde uh-e_B)u=0,
$$
there is a morphism $k:C\to E_B$ such that
$$
kp=\widetilde uh-e_B.
$$
Writing $\overline u:Q_A\to Q_B$ for the induced morphism, precomposition
with $p$ gives
$$
\overline u\,\overline h=\pi_Bk.
$$
It follows that
$$
\Sigma G(u)\partial_\xi
=\rho_BG(\overline u\,\overline h)
=\rho_BG(\pi_Bk)=0.
$$
Finally,
$$
\Sigma G(p)\Sigma G(u)=\Sigma G(pu)=0.
$$
Thus $(G,\Sigma G)$ is a graded connecting functor on $\{0,1\}$.

We next prove effaceability. To compute $\Sigma G(e_A)$, use
$e_{E_A}:E_A\to E_{E_A}$ itself as a lift. The induced morphism
$Q_A\to Q_{E_A}$ is zero, since its composite with the epimorphism $\pi_A$
is
$$
\pi_{E_A}e_{E_A}=0.
$$
Hence $\Sigma G(e_A)=0$, and therefore $\Sigma G$ is effaceable.

It remains to prove universality. Let
$$
T=(T^0,T^1,\partial_T)
$$
be a graded connecting functor on $\{0,1\}$ and let
$f^0:G\to T^0$ be a natural transformation. For the canonical short exact
sequence
$$
\xi_A:\quad
0\longrightarrow A\xrightarrow{e_A}E_A\xrightarrow{\pi_A}Q_A
\longrightarrow0,
$$
the composite
$$
G(Q_A)\xrightarrow{f^0_{Q_A}}T^0(Q_A)
\xrightarrow{\partial_{T,\xi_A}}T^1(A)
$$
kills the image of $G(\pi_A)$, because
$$
\partial_{T,\xi_A}f^0_{Q_A}G(\pi_A)
=
\partial_{T,\xi_A}T^0(\pi_A)f^0_{E_A}=0.
$$
There is consequently a unique morphism
$$
f^1_A:\Sigma G(A)\longrightarrow T^1(A)
$$
such that
$$
f^1_A\rho_A=\partial_{T,\xi_A}f^0_{Q_A}.
$$

These morphisms are natural. For $f:A\to B$, a chosen lift
$\widetilde f$ and its induced $\overline f$ form a morphism
$\xi_A\to\xi_B$. Naturality of the boundary of $T$ gives
\begin{align*}
T^1(f)f^1_A\rho_A
&=T^1(f)\partial_{T,\xi_A}f^0_{Q_A}\\
&=\partial_{T,\xi_B}T^0(\overline f)f^0_{Q_A}\\
&=\partial_{T,\xi_B}f^0_{Q_B}G(\overline f)\\
&=f^1_B\rho_BG(\overline f)\\
&=f^1_B\Sigma G(f)\rho_A.
\end{align*}
Since $\rho_A$ is an epimorphism, this proves naturality.

For an arbitrary short exact sequence $\xi$ as above, the chosen maps
$$
(\mathrm{id}_A,h,\overline h):\xi\longrightarrow\xi_A
$$
form a morphism of short exact sequences. Therefore
\begin{align*}
f^1_A\partial_\xi
&=f^1_A\rho_AG(\overline h)\\
&=\partial_{T,\xi_A}f^0_{Q_A}G(\overline h)\\
&=\partial_{T,\xi_A}T^0(\overline h)f^0_C\\
&=\partial_{T,\xi}f^0_C.
\end{align*}
Thus $(f^0,f^1)$ is a morphism of graded connecting functors.

For $\xi_A$, we may use $h=\mathrm{id}_{E_A}$, and then
$\overline h=\mathrm{id}_{Q_A}$. Hence the boundary of the constructed
pair for $\xi_A$ is $\rho_A$. Boundary compatibility therefore forces
every possible $f^1_A$ to satisfy
$$
f^1_A\rho_A=\partial_{T,\xi_A}f^0_{Q_A}.
$$
Since $\rho_A$ is an epimorphism, $f^1$ is unique. This proves
universality.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Existence of right satellites]
\label{lemma-right-satellites-injective-effacements}
\begin{reference}
\cite[Chapter II, \S 2.2, Theorem 2.2.2, pp. 141--142]{Tohoku}
\end{reference}
Let $\mathcal A$ and $\mathcal B$ be abelian categories, and suppose every
object of $\mathcal A$ has an injective effacement. For every covariant
additive functor
$$
F:\mathcal A\longrightarrow\mathcal B
$$
there exists a universal graded connecting functor of step $+1$
$$
S=(S^nF,\partial^n)_{n\geq0}
$$
whose degree-zero functor is $S^0F=F$. Every $S^nF$, $n>0$, is
effaceable. Hence finite right satellite systems exist through every
positive degree.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Put $S^0F=F$ and recursively set
$$
S^{n+1}F=\Sigma(S^nF).
$$
Let $\partial^n$ be the boundary supplied by
Lemma \ref{lemma-first-right-satellite-injective-effacements}. For every
$n\geq0$, after reindexing degrees $n,n+1$ as $0,1$, the pair
$$
(S^nF,S^{n+1}F,\partial^n)
$$
is a graded connecting functor. Every adjacent composite in the infinite
associated string occurs in one of these two-degree strings. The
naturality and complex conditions for the pairs therefore show that the
whole collection $S$ is a graded connecting functor on the nonnegative
integers. Moreover,
$$
S^nF=\Sigma(S^{n-1}F)
$$
is effaceable for every $n>0$.

Let $T=(T^n,\partial_T^n)_{n\geq0}$ be any graded connecting functor on the
nonnegative integers and let
$$
f^0:F\longrightarrow T^0
$$
be a natural transformation. Suppose $f^n:S^nF\to T^n$ has been
constructed. Reindex the adjacent portion $(T^n,T^{n+1})$ as a graded
connecting functor on $\{0,1\}$. Universality of the pair
$(S^nF,S^{n+1}F)$ extends $f^n$ to a unique natural transformation
$$
f^{n+1}:S^{n+1}F\longrightarrow T^{n+1}
$$
for which the boundary square in degree $n$ commutes. Induction produces a
morphism $f:S\to T$. If another such morphism has degree-zero component
$f^0$, the uniqueness clause for the pair in degrees $0,1$ gives equality
in degree $1$, and the corresponding clause in each successive pair gives
equality in every degree. Thus $S$ is universal.

The same finite induction proves that, for every $a>0$, the restriction
$$
S|_{\{0,\ldots,a\}}
$$
is universal. With its identity degree-zero identification it is therefore
a right satellite system of length $a$ for $F$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Finite effaceability criterion]
\label{lemma-finite-effaceability-universality}
\begin{reference}
\cite[Chapter II, \S 2.2, Proposition 2.2.1, p. 141]{Tohoku}
\end{reference}
\begin{history}
The printed proposition declares the graded connecting functor to be
covariant or contravariant, but then applies the covariant
effaceability and injective-effacement clauses without distinguishing
the two variances. We use the registered corrected reading: the
covariant case uses effaceability and injective effacements, whereas
the contravariant case uses co-effaceability and projective
effacements. We also use the registered correction of the inconsistent
printed index range to $0\leq i\leq a$. The source treats the
two-degree extension and invokes induction; the proof below records
the complete finite induction under the unpadded interval convention
of Definition \ref{definition-graded-connecting-functor}.
\end{history}
Let $\mathcal A$ and $\mathcal B$ be abelian categories, let $a>0$,
and put
$$
I_a=\{0,\ldots,a\}.
$$
Let
$$
T=(T^n,\partial_T^n)_{0\leq n\leq a}
$$
be an exact graded connecting functor of step $+1$ on $I_a$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item Suppose $T$ is covariant. If $T^n$ is effaceable for every
$0<n\leq a$, then $T$ is universal. Conversely, if every object of
$\mathcal A$ has an injective effacement and $T$ is universal, then
$T^n$ is effaceable for every $0<n\leq a$.

\item Suppose $T$ is contravariant. Regard each $T^n$ as an additive
functor
$$
T^n:\mathcal A^{opp}\longrightarrow\mathcal B.
$$
If these functors are effaceable for every $0<n\leq a$, then $T$ is
universal. Conversely, if every object of $\mathcal A^{opp}$ has an
injective effacement and $T$ is universal, then these functors are
effaceable for every $0<n\leq a$.
\end{enumerate}
Equivalently, in the language of $\mathcal A$, effaceability of
$T^n:\mathcal A^{opp}\to\mathcal B$ means that for every object $A$
there is an epimorphism $p:P\to A$ such that
$$
T^n(p):T^n(A)\longrightarrow T^n(P)
$$
is zero; this is the contravariant co-effaceability condition.
Injective effacements in $\mathcal A^{opp}$ are precisely projective
effacements in $\mathcal A$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
We first prove (1). Let
$$
U=(U^i,\partial_U^i)_{0\leq i\leq a}
$$
be a covariant graded connecting functor on $I_a$, and let
$f^0:T^0\to U^0$ be a natural transformation. We construct the remaining
components by induction.

Suppose that, for some $0\leq n<a$, natural transformations
$$
f^i:T^i\longrightarrow U^i,\qquad 0\leq i\leq n,
$$
have been constructed and are compatible with the boundaries through
degree $n-1$. For every object $A$, choose a monomorphism
$$
u_A:A\longrightarrow M_A
$$
such that $T^{n+1}(u_A)=0$, put $Q_A=\Coker(u_A)$, and denote the resulting
short exact sequence by
$$
\xi_A:\quad
0\longrightarrow A\xrightarrow{u_A}M_A\xrightarrow{q_A}Q_A
\longrightarrow0.
$$
Exactness of $T$ gives an exact sequence
$$
T^n(M_A)\xrightarrow{T^n(q_A)}T^n(Q_A)
\xrightarrow{\partial^n_{T,\xi_A}}T^{n+1}(A)
\xrightarrow{T^{n+1}(u_A)}T^{n+1}(M_A).
$$
The last arrow is zero. Hence $\partial^n_{T,\xi_A}$ is an epimorphism
and
$$
\Ker(\partial^n_{T,\xi_A})
=
\Im(T^n(q_A)).
$$
In other words, $\partial^n_{T,\xi_A}$ is a cokernel of $T^n(q_A)$.

The morphism
$$
\partial^n_{U,\xi_A}\circ f^n_{Q_A}:
T^n(Q_A)\longrightarrow U^{n+1}(A)
$$
kills the image of $T^n(q_A)$, since
\begin{align*}
\partial^n_{U,\xi_A}f^n_{Q_A}T^n(q_A)
&=
\partial^n_{U,\xi_A}U^n(q_A)f^n_{M_A}\\
&=0.
\end{align*}
There is therefore a unique morphism
$$
f^{n+1}_{A,u_A}:T^{n+1}(A)\longrightarrow U^{n+1}(A)
$$
such that
\begin{equation}
\label{equation-finite-effaceability-factor}
f^{n+1}_{A,u_A}\partial^n_{T,\xi_A}
=
\partial^n_{U,\xi_A}f^n_{Q_A}.
\end{equation}

We first show that this morphism is independent of $u_A$. Let
$u:A\to M$ and $u':A\to M'$ be two monomorphisms killed by $T^{n+1}$.
Form the pushout
$$
\xymatrix{
A \ar[r]^u \ar[d]_{u'} &
M \ar[d]^v \\
M' \ar[r]_{v'} &
P.
}
$$
Put $w=vu=v'u'$. Pushouts preserve monomorphisms in an abelian
category, so $w$ is a monomorphism, and
$$
T^{n+1}(w)=T^{n+1}(v)T^{n+1}(u)=0.
$$
The maps $v$ and $v'$ give morphisms from the short exact sequences
defined by $u$ and $u'$ to the one defined by $w$. If
$\overline v:\Coker(u)\to\Coker(w)$ is the map induced by $v$, then
naturality of the boundaries and of $f^n$ gives
\begin{align*}
f^{n+1}_{A,w}\partial^n_{T,\xi_u}
&=
f^{n+1}_{A,w}\partial^n_{T,\xi_w}T^n(\overline v)\\
&=
\partial^n_{U,\xi_w}f^n_{\Coker(w)}T^n(\overline v)\\
&=
\partial^n_{U,\xi_w}U^n(\overline v)f^n_{\Coker(u)}\\
&=
\partial^n_{U,\xi_u}f^n_{\Coker(u)}\\
&=
f^{n+1}_{A,u}\partial^n_{T,\xi_u}.
\end{align*}
Since $\partial^n_{T,\xi_u}$ is an epimorphism,
$f^{n+1}_{A,w}=f^{n+1}_{A,u}$. The same argument with $u'$ proves
independence. We may consequently write the resulting morphism simply
as $f^{n+1}_A$.

We next prove naturality. Let $\alpha:A\to B$. Push out an effacing
monomorphism $u:A\to M$ along $\alpha$:
$$
\xymatrix{
A \ar[r]^u \ar[d]_{\alpha} &
M \ar[d]^v \\
B \ar[r]_j &
P.
}
$$
The morphism $j$ is a monomorphism. Choose a monomorphism
$e:P\to N$ such that $T^{n+1}(e)=0$. Then $ej:B\to N$ is a
monomorphism killed by $T^{n+1}$. The maps $\alpha$ and $ev$ induce a
morphism of short exact sequences
$$
(\alpha,ev,\overline\alpha):\xi_u\longrightarrow\xi_{ej}.
$$
Using independence to compute $f^{n+1}_B$ with the effacement $ej$, we
obtain
\begin{align*}
U^{n+1}(\alpha)f^{n+1}_A\partial^n_{T,\xi_u}
&=
U^{n+1}(\alpha)\partial^n_{U,\xi_u}f^n_{\Coker(u)}\\
&=
\partial^n_{U,\xi_{ej}}U^n(\overline\alpha)
f^n_{\Coker(u)}\\
&=
\partial^n_{U,\xi_{ej}}f^n_{\Coker(ej)}
T^n(\overline\alpha)\\
&=
f^{n+1}_B\partial^n_{T,\xi_{ej}}T^n(\overline\alpha)\\
&=
f^{n+1}_BT^{n+1}(\alpha)\partial^n_{T,\xi_u}.
\end{align*}
Again $\partial^n_{T,\xi_u}$ is an epimorphism, so
$$
U^{n+1}(\alpha)f^{n+1}_A
=
f^{n+1}_BT^{n+1}(\alpha).
$$
Thus $f^{n+1}$ is a natural transformation.

It remains to verify compatibility with every boundary in degree $n$.
Let
$$
\zeta:\quad
0\longrightarrow A\xrightarrow{i}B\xrightarrow{p}C
\longrightarrow0
$$
be a short exact sequence. Choose a monomorphism $e:B\to N$ such that
$T^{n+1}(e)=0$. Then $ei:A\to N$ is a monomorphism killed by
$T^{n+1}$. If $R=\Coker(ei)$, there is an induced morphism of short
exact sequences
$$
(\mathrm{id}_A,e,\overline e):\zeta\longrightarrow\xi_{ei}.
$$
Computing $f^{n+1}_A$ using $ei$, we find
\begin{align*}
f^{n+1}_A\partial^n_{T,\zeta}
&=
f^{n+1}_A\partial^n_{T,\xi_{ei}}T^n(\overline e)\\
&=
\partial^n_{U,\xi_{ei}}f^n_RT^n(\overline e)\\
&=
\partial^n_{U,\xi_{ei}}U^n(\overline e)f^n_C\\
&=
\partial^n_{U,\zeta}f^n_C.
\end{align*}
This proves boundary compatibility.

Finally, any component in degree $n+1$ compatible with the boundaries
must, upon applying it to $\xi_A$, satisfy
Equation \ref{equation-finite-effaceability-factor}. Since
$\partial^n_{T,\xi_A}$ is an epimorphism, that component is unique.
Induction for $n=0,\ldots,a-1$ therefore gives a unique morphism
$T\to U$ extending $f^0$. Thus $T$ is universal. Notice that at the
last step exactness is used only at $T^{a-1}(Q_A)$ and $T^a(A)$;
both terms have an incoming and an outgoing arrow in the unpadded
finite string.

Conversely, suppose every object of $\mathcal A$ has an injective
effacement and $T$ is universal. Apply
Lemma \ref{lemma-right-satellites-injective-effacements} to $T^0$ and
restrict the resulting right-satellite system to $I_a$. The restriction
is universal and all its positive components are effaceable. Lemma
\ref{lemma-uniqueness-universal-graded-connecting-functor} gives an
isomorphism from $T$ to this restriction whose degree-zero component is
the identity. Effaceability is preserved by natural isomorphism, proving
the converse in (1).

For (2), use Lemma
\ref{lemma-graded-connecting-functor-dualities} to regard $T$ as a
covariant exact graded connecting functor on $\mathcal A^{opp}$.
The notions of morphism and universality are unchanged under this
identification, so (1) applies. A monomorphism in $\mathcal A^{opp}$ is
an epimorphism in $\mathcal A$, and an injective effacement in
$\mathcal A^{opp}$ is a projective effacement in $\mathcal A$. This is
exactly the stated co-effaceable/projective form.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Exactness criterion for right satellites]
\label{lemma-right-satellites-exactness-criterion}
\begin{reference}
\cite[Chapter II, \S 2.2, Theorem 2.2.2, p. 142]{Tohoku}
\end{reference}
Let $\mathcal A$ and $\mathcal B$ be abelian categories, and suppose every
